Overview 
1 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony in 
2 
these proceedings? 
3 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to 
4 
address: (1) the Rate Year, or RY, July 1, 2024 
5 
through June 30, 2025, electric and gas delivery 
6 
revenues at current rates; (2) the Company’s 
7 
Embedded Cost of Service studies, referred to as 
8 
the ECOS studies; (3) the Company’s electric and 
9 
gas revenue allocations; (4) electric and gas 
10 
rate design and bill impacts; (5) Central 
11 
Hudson’s proposed new and modified surcharges; 
12 
and (6) the Company and Staff’s additional 
13 
proposed tariff modifications.  
14 
Q. 
In your testimony, will the Panel refer to, or 
15 
otherwise rely upon, any information produced 
16 
during the discovery phase of these proceedings? 
17 
A. 
Yes, we will refer to, and have relied upon, 
18 
several responses to Staff’s and other parties’ 
19 
Information Requests, referred to as IRs.  These 
20 
responses are included in Exhibit___(SRP-1).  We 
21 
will refer to them by the party requesting the 
22 
information, and the numbering of that request, 
23 
for example, DPS-123. 
24 
4239

Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
5 
 
Q. 
Are you sponsoring any other exhibits? 
1 
A. 
Yes, the Panel is sponsoring other exhibits.  
2 
Exhibit___(Updated SRP-2) contains Staff’s RY 
3 
electric and gas customers and sales forecast 
4 
priced out at current rates.  Exhibit___(Updated 
5 
SRP-3) contains our recommended electric and gas 
6 
revenue allocation.  Exhibit___(Updated SPR-4) 
7 
contains a summary of current and proposed 
8 
rates.  Exhibit___(Updated SRP-5) contains our 
9 
estimated bill impacts using Staff’s recommended 
10 
revenue requirement.   
11 
Rate Year Operating Revenue at Current Rates 
12 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company determined the 
13 
amount of delivery revenue in the Rate Year at 
14 
current rates. 
15 
A. 
The Company’s Rate Year forecast for delivery 
16 
revenue contains: (1) customer and sales revenue 
17 
group forecasts, and (2) post model adjustments 
18 
that consist of heat pump, electric vehicle, or 
19 
EV, and photovoltaic, or PV, adjustments.  The 
20 
Company uses historic data to allocate its 
21 
customer and sales revenue group forecasts to 
22 
each service classification, referred to as S.C. 
23 
or service class.  The allocated monthly 
24 
4240


--- Page 17 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
6 
 
customers and sales, or billing determinants, 
1 
are then priced out at the existing rates of 
2 
each service class.  The Company then applies 
3 
its service class specific customers and sales 
4 
related to its forecasted heat pumps, EV, and 
5 
PVs, and prices out the expected increase or 
6 
lost revenue for each post model adjustment.  
7 
The summation of these components priced out at 
8 
current rates results in the Company’s annual 
9 
Rate Year delivery revenue prior to any rate 
10 
increase. 
11 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Company’s 
12 
approach? 
13 
A. 
Yes, it is a reasonable approach. 
14 
Q. 
Did the Panel develop a price-out model to 
15 
allocate customers and sales volumes for both 
16 
electric and gas? 
17 
A. 
The Panel configured its own price-out models 
18 
using the Company’s workpapers, which included 
19 
the use of the Company’s historic ratios that 
20 
allocate customers and sales from customer 
21 
revenue groups to service classes as well as the 
22 
Company’s individual service class files that 
23 
price out monthly forecasted sales and customers 
24 
4241


--- Page 18 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
7 
 
at current rates.  The Panel’s models are a 
1 
slightly more simplified version of the 
2 
Company’s models, which consists of numerous 
3 
Excel files that collate and calculate data for 
4 
various pieces of revenue.   
5 
Q. 
Did the Panel verify the Company’s results? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  After incorporating the Company’s RY 
7 
customer and sales forecasts into the Panel’s 
8 
electric and gas price-out models, our revenue 
9 
output closely matched Central Hudson’s results.  
10 
The Panel’s RY revenues were within 0.01 percent 
11 
of the Company’s RY electric delivery revenues 
12 
and gas delivery revenues.   
13 
 Q. Does the Panel have its own RY customer and 
14 
sales forecast? 
15 
A. 
Yes, the Staff Forecasting Panel provided a RY 
16 
customer and sales forecast for both electric 
17 
and gas.  The electric forecast includes a 
18 
customer and sales forecast for each of eight 
19 
revenue groups: Residential Non-demand, 
20 
Residential Demand, Commercial Non-demand, 
21 
Commercial Demand, Industrial Non-Demand, 
22 
Industrial Demand, Other Public Authority or OPA 
23 
Non-Demand, and OPA Demand.  The gas forecast 
24 
4242


--- Page 19 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
8 
 
has six revenue groups including: Residential 
1 
Heat, Residential Non-heat, Commercial Heat, 
2 
Commercial Non-heat, Industrial and OPA.   
3 
Q. 
Did the Panel include any post model adjustments 
4 
such as EVs, heat pumps and PV net metering in 
5 
its electric or gas revenue price outs? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  The post model adjustments can be found in 
7 
Exhibit___(Updated SRP-2), Schedule A and B.   
8 
 Q. Besides using Staff’s RY customer and sales 
9 
forecasts, did the Panel make any changes from 
10 
the Company’s price-out methodology for how it 
11 
priced out revenues in the RY?  
12 
A. 
Yes, for the gas revenue price-out.   
13 
Q. 
Please explain why this was done. 
14 
A. 
The Company’s gas service class S.C. No. 6, for 
15 
Central Hudson’s firm customers transporting at 
16 
least 35,000 centum cubic feet or ccf per year 
17 
of customer-owned gas, has a high-volume 
18 
discount rate for customers with an annual usage 
19 
above 50,000 ccf.  These customers are billed on 
20 
a high-volume discount rate, currently $0.3869 
21 
per ccf.  The Company’s gas model incorrectly 
22 
priced out these customers on the regular block 
23 
rate structure for S.C. No. 2, for commercial 
24 
4243


--- Page 20 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
9 
 
and industrial customers; S.C. No. 6; and S.C. 
1 
No. 13, for aggregated firm commercial and 
2 
industrial transportation customers.  This 
3 
approach produced higher revenues than the use 
4 
of the discount rate and is not a correct 
5 
representation of what revenue would be produced 
6 
under current rates.  This was also confirmed by 
7 
the Company in its publicly available response 
8 
to DPS-697. 
9 
Q. 
Did the Panel adjust its price-out to account 
10 
for these high-volume usage customers in S.C. 
11 
No. 6? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  Historically, S.C. No. 6 has a low number 
13 
of high-volume customers who would fall under 
14 
the high-volume discount rate but use a 
15 
relatively large amount of gas.  Based on the 
16 
information included in the non-confidential 
17

8 
Embedded Cost of Service Studies 
9 
Q. 
Please briefly describe the purpose of an ECOS 
10 
study. 
11 
A. 
An ECOS study provides a comparative analysis of 
12 
the cost of providing utility services to each 
13 
customer class, and the rate of return, or ROR, 
14 
from each class.  It is based on an analysis of 
15 
the rate base, operating expenses, and revenues 
16 
for a prior calendar year or forecasted RY.  The 
17 
three major steps in an ECOS study are 
18 
functionalization and classification of costs to 
19 
operating functions, and allocation of those 
20 
costs to customer classes. 
21 
Q. 
What does an ECOS study provide? 
22 
A. 
The final output of an ECOS study contains a 
23 
summary of the individual class rates of return, 
24 
4246


--- Page 23 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
12 
 
which indicates the level each class contributes 
1 
to the total system rate of return.  The rate of 
2 
return for a class is an effective way to 
3 
measure how well the class revenues align to the 
4 
Company’s costs to provide service to that 
5 
particular customer class.  The study also 
6 
provides an analysis to establish the 
7 
appropriate revenue targets for bill printing, 
8 
mailing and receipt services, merchant function 
9 
charge, referred to as MFC, uncollectibles, and 
10 
credit and collection functions.   
11 
Q. 
What is a “tolerance band” and why is it used?  
12 
A. 
A tolerance band is a certain percentage above 
13 
and below the system average rate of return 
14 
determined through the ECOS study.  Service 
15 
classes are considered deficient or in surplus 
16 
if the service class’s specific rate of return 
17 
falls outside the tolerance band.  The purpose 
18 
of a tolerance band is to account for the 
19 
imprecise nature of an ECOS study.  Central 
20 
Hudson proposes to use a tolerance band of 15 
21 
percent for electric and gas.   
22 
Q. 
Did the Company file an electric and gas ECOS 
23 
study in these proceedings? 
24 
4247


--- Page 24 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
13 
 
A. 
Yes, the Company filed three electric and three 
1 
gas ECOS studies in these proceedings: a 
2 
historical ECOS study based on the 12 months 
3 
ended December 31, 2021, as shown in Company 
4 
Exhibit___(COSP-1), Schedule A for electric and 
5 
Exhibit___(COSP-2), Schedule A for gas; a pro 
6 
forma ECOS study based on the 12 months ending 
7 
June 30, 2025, as shown in Company 
8 
Exhibit___(COSP-1), Schedules B and C for 
9 
electric and Company Exhibit___(COSP-2), 
10 
Schedules B and C for gas; and a hypothetical 
11 
electric and gas delivery only ECOS study based 
12 
on the 12 months ended December 31, 2021 as 
13 
shown in Company Exhibit___(COSP-3). 
14

15 
for its electric and gas systems? 
16 
A. 
As stated on page 8 of the Company’s Cost of 
17 
Service Panel testimony, the Company’s historic 
18 
and pro forma studies allow for a comparison of 
19 
estimated realized to estimated expected rates 
20 
of return based on the current rate structure.  
21 
The electric and gas historical ECOS studies are 
22 
based on the actual recorded data for the 2021 
23 
calendar year and the electric and gas pro forma 
24 
4248


--- Page 25 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
14 
 
ECOS studies are based on the Company’s 
1 
forecasted RY.  The Company uses both historic 
2 
and pro forma studies for the basis of its 
3 
electric and gas revenue allocation.  On page 25 
4 
of the Company’s Cost of Service Panel 
5 
testimony, the Company explains that while the 
6 
hypothetical study is not used for revenue 
7 
allocation proposes, it does provide a valuable 
8 
connection between the bundled commodity and 
9 
delivery historic study and its delivery-only 
10 
pro forma study.

of Service Panel explained, on page 12 of its

20

3 
Tariff Modifications 
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel recommend any tariff adjustments 
5 
based on what the Company has proposed? 
6 
A. 
Yes, specifically regarding the Company’s 
7 
proposal on page 56 of the Company’s Revenue 
8 
Requirement Panel testimony where it discusses a 
9 
recovery or refund due to rate change timing.  
10 
However, the Panel believes this issue can be 
11 
addressed through the Company’s Revenue 
12 
Decoupling Mechanism, or RDM. 
13 
Q. 
What is an RDM? 
14 
A. 
The RDM is a recovery or pass back mechanism 
15 
that reconciles the Company’s actual billed 
16 
revenues to revenue targets, which are based on 
17 
the Company’s base rates multiplied by its 
18 
monthly customers and sales forecasts.  The 
19 
actual monthly billed revenue is compared to the 
20 
monthly revenue targets, where any over or under 
21 
collections are passed back to or recovered from 
22 
customers.  Reconciling actual billed revenues 
23 
to the established RDM targes allows the Company 
24 
4271


--- Page 48 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
37 
 
to fully recover its RY revenue requirement.    
1 
Q. 
Please explain the Company’s proposal regarding 
2 
a recovery or refund due to rate change timing. 
3 
A. 
The Company explains how there is a disconnect 
4 
between the revenue requirement derived in its 
5 
rate design, which uses a full twelve months of 
6 
sales to design the new rates to take effect 
7 
July 1, and the actual billed revenue it 
8 
receives in July, which would comprise of a 
9 
blend of the old and new rates.  According to 
10 
Central Hudson’s response to DPS-590, the 
11 
Company has included, in its revenue 
12 
requirements, $5,480,000 to be recovered from 
13 
electric customers and $596,000 to be recovered 
14 
from gas customers.  The Company states in its 
15 
response to DPS-590 that “the Company calculated 
16 
the rate allowance for this cost element by 
17 
applying a factor to the proposed rate increases 
18 
for electric and gas.  The use of a factor 
19 
results in the allowance for this cost element 
20 
to remain proportionate to the eventual rates 
21 
approved in these proceedings.” 
22 
Q. 
Do you agree with the Company? 
23 
A. 
No.  The RDM targets developed assume a full 
24 
4272


--- Page 49 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
38 
 
twelve months of forecasted sales will be priced 
1 
out at the new RY rates.  Any over- or under-
2 
collection from the Company’s actual revenues 
3 
comparative to the RDM target in a month where a 
4 
rate change occurs would be corrected by the RDM 
5 
reconciliation mechanism.  However, the Panel 
6 
does recognize the Company has, in its electric 
7 
tariff on Leaf 163.5.4, and in its gas tariff on 
8 
Leaf 129, language requiring the Company to 
9 
adjust its actual billed revenue by multiplying 
10 
the actual billed sales in the month of a rate 
11 
increase by the new rates.  This would 
12 
effectively reverse any proration that would 
13 
occur in its actual billed revenues.  For months 
14 
where a Company’s rates would increase, this 
15 
provision essentially overstates the Company’s 
16 
actual billed revenue and would not be fully 
17 
reconciled in the RDM for the month a rate 
18 
change occurs. 
19 
Q. 
What does the Panel recommend regarding this 
20 
rate change timing issue? 
21 
A. 
We recommend that the Company’s electric and gas 
22 
tariffs be modified to eliminate this existing 
23 
tariff language requiring the Company to reverse 
24 
4273


--- Page 50 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Rates Panel 
 
 
39 
 
any proration that would occur in its actual 
1 
billed revenue for the months in which a rate 
2 
change occurs.  The Company should be able to 
3 
reconcile its actual billed revenue to the RDM 
4 
targets in order to fully recover its RY revenue 
5 
requirement.  Additionally, there would be no 
6 
need to assume a revenue requirement adjustment 
7 
in base rates to recover any assumed shortfall 
8 
of delivery revenue the Company is proposing, as 
9 
the RDM would correct any shortfall or excess 
10 
revenue based on the established monthly RDM 
11 
targets.   
12 
Q. 
What is the Excelsior Jobs Program, or EJP? 
13 
A. 
The EJP is a program that provides qualifying 
14 
customers with discounted electric rates to 
15 
encourage economic development.  The discounted 
16 
rates are based on the utility’s marginal cost 
17 
of service. 
18 
Q. 
Did the Company modify its current EJP rates? 
19 
A. 
Yes.  Page 65 of the Company’s Forecasting and 
20

21 
updating the EJP delivery rates based on the 
22 
results of its recent marginal cost of service 
23 
study. 
24 
4274

3 
Q. 
How should property taxes be functionalized?
4 
A. 
Property tax obligations are determined by using
5 
the depreciated current cost to reconstruct
6 
improvements, plus land value, generally denoted
7 
as Reproduction Cost New Less Depreciation plus
8 
Land.  Therefore, property taxes should be
9 
functionalized based on net plant in service as
10 
net plant in service should serve as a
11 
reasonable proxy for Reproduction Cost New Less 
12 
Depreciation plus Land.

22

23

24

4288

Billing System Transition 
7 
Q. 
Describe the Company’s CIS billing transition.  
8 
A. 
On page 9 of the Customer Experience Panel’s 
9 
initial testimony, the Company states it 
10 
launched a new SAP-based customer information 
11 
system, or CIS, on September 1, 2021.  SAP is a 
12 
German multinational software company that 
13 
develops and markets enterprise software to 
14 
manage business operations and customer 
15 
relations.  The Company states that following 
16 
the launch of its new CIS, several issues arose 
17 
that impacted “a portion of Central Hudson’s 
18 
customers, including through delayed and 
19 
inaccurate invoices, confusion, and anxiety.”  
20 
The Company states it put forth its “best 
21 
efforts to correct the issues,” but experienced 
22 
an increase in customer complaints and “harsh 
23 
criticism from politicians.”  In March of 2022, 
24 
4334


--- Page 111 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
17 
 
the Public Service Commission, or Commission, 
1 
announced an investigation into customer service 
2 
and customer billing issues at the Company.  The 
3 
Department’s Office of Investigations and 
4 
Enforcement, or OIE Staff, filed a report 
5 
detailing its findings on December 15, 2022, 
6 
referred to herein as the Staff Investigation 
7 
Report.

Q. 
What is the difference between an initial and an 
8 
escalated complaint? 
9 
A.  As described in the Department’s Office of 
10 
Consumer Services Monthly Report on Consumer 
11 
Complaint Activity filed under Matter 19-00950, 
12 
there are two types of complaints: initial 
13 
complaints and escalated complaints.  All 
14 
complaints begin as initial complaints.  Initial 
15 
complaints are escalated if the customer pro-
16 
actively informs the Department that the utility 
17 
failed to satisfy their complaint.  If the 
18 
customer does not so inform the Department, then 
19 
their case may be closed, notwithstanding that 
20 
they may remain dissatisfied with the utility’s 
21 
response.

1 
Company has a strong incentive to prioritize its 
2

4

5 
Customer Service Performance Indicators 
6 
Q. 
What is the purpose of annual customer service 
7 
performance indicator mechanisms? 
8 
A. 
Customer service performance indicators, or 
9 
CSPIs, are data points used by OCS Staff to 
10 
measure the utilities’ levels of customer 
11 
service so the Commission can determine whether 
12 
the utilities are providing adequate customer 
13 
service to their customers.  These CSPI 
14 
mechanisms help to align shareholder and 
15 
ratepayer interests by providing earnings 
16 
consequences related to the quality of service 
17 
that a utility provides to its customers.  The 
18 
mechanism links earnings directly to a utility’s 
19 
performance on specific measures of customer 
20 
service.  Based on historical performance, the 
21 
Commission establishes target levels for metrics 
22 
during utility rate cases.  Currently, such 
23 
mechanisms are in effect for most of New York’s 
24 
4342


--- Page 119 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
25 
 
major investor-owned utilities, including 
1 
Consolidated Edison of New York, Inc., or Con 
2 
Edison, Corning, KEDLI, KEDNY, Niagara Mohawk, 
3 
NYSEG, RG&E, and Orange and Rockland Utilities, 
4 
Inc., or Orange and Rockland. 
5 
Q. 
Why are CSPIs necessary? 
6 
A. 
As monopoly providers of utility delivery 
7 
services, investor-owned utilities, or IOUs, 
8 
such as Central Hudson, do not have a profit-
9 
based incentive to provide adequate customer 
10 
service because their customers cannot select 
11 
another utility based on the quality of service 
12 
received.  However, a primary responsibility of 
13 
the utilities is to provide adequate customer 
14 
service and this is in the public interest.  A 
15 
CSPI mechanism establishes an incentive for 
16 
utilities to provide adequate levels of customer 
17 
service. 
18 
Q. 
How are CSPIs monitored by the Department?  
19 
A. 
OCS Staff uses CSPI reports filed annually and 
20 
monthly by the utilities to measure and monitor 
21 
customer service. 
22 
Q. 
What do the monthly CSPI reports entail? 
23 
A. 
The monthly CSPIs are a standardized set of 
24 
4343


--- Page 120 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
26 
 
measures for customer service performance that 
1 
are reported by all major IOUs.  These measures 
2 
are reported on a monthly basis, facilitating 
3 
comparative analysis of customer service across 
4 
the utilities.  This allows OCS Staff to 
5 
identify trends in utility performances 
6 
throughout the year. 
7 
Q. 
What is the difference between the annual and 
8 
monthly CSPIs? 
9 
A. 
The annual CSPIs are reported once per year, 
10 
along with being established, modified, and 
11 
approved within a utility’s rate case and have 
12 
earnings incentives, also known as negative 
13 
revenue adjustments, or NRAs, which are tied to 
14 
a utility’s performance results.  Currently, 
15 
performance targets are set for the yearly CSPI 
16 
metrics, and Department Staff uses the monthly 
17 
CSPI reports to verify the accuracy of the 
18 
annually reported CSPI results. 
19 
Q. 
Describe Central Hudson’s current annual CSPIs. 
20 
A. 
As shown on pages 9-12 of the Company’s Customer 
21 
Experience Panel’s initial testimony, Central 
22 
Hudson’s current annual CSPI measures the 
23 
following areas: PSC Complaint Rate, based on 
24 
4344


--- Page 121 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
27 
 
the average number of escalated complaints per 
1 
100,000 customers; Customer Satisfaction, 
2 
measured through a residential customer 
3 
satisfaction survey; and Percent of Calls 
4 
Answered Within 30 Seconds, or Call Answer Rate, 
5 
measured by the percent of calls answered within 
6 
30 seconds by a customer service representative.  
7 
The current minimum targets are a PSC Complaint 
8 
Rate less than or equal to 1.0; greater than or 
9 
equal to 89 percent for the Customer 
10 
Satisfaction Survey; and greater than or equal 
11 
to 67 percent for the Call Answer Rate metric. 
12 
Q. 
If performance on any of the three CSPI metrics 
13 
listed above falls below the specified 
14 
thresholds, will the Company incur an NRA? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  The Company is currently at risk of 
16 
incurring a total of 42 pre-tax combined basis 
17 
points, which is equivalent to approximately 
18 
$5.5 million, in potential electric and gas NRAs 
19 
annually.  These basis points are divided among 
20 
three of the performance measures: PSC Complaint 
21 
Rate, which has 15 basis points at risk; 
22 
Residential Customer Satisfaction Survey, which 
23 
has 15 basis points at risk; and Percent of 
24 
4345


--- Page 122 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
28 
 
Calls Answered by a Representative within 30 
1 
seconds, which has 12 basis points at risk.

23

24 
4346

3 
Q. 
Please describe the Panel’s traditional 
4 
methodology for recommending metric targets. 
5 
A. 
In previous rate cases, the Commission has 
6 
accepted that historical averages generally 
7 
represent a satisfactory level of service.  To 
8 
set the metric targets, Staff typically 
9 
calculates a utility’s historical average 
10 
performance using between 36 and 60 months of 
11 
data.  Staff then sets the minimum target two 
12 
standard deviations below that historical 
13 
average. 
14 
Q. 
What is the source of the 60 months of 
15 
historical data? 
16 
A. 
We relied upon the monthly CSPI reports that 
17 
Central Hudson filed in Case 15-M-0566.  The 
18 
Company also stated in its response to DPS-356 
19 
that it filed revised CSPI reports on March 23, 
20 
2023 in Case 15-M-0566 for the 17 months from 
21 
September 2021 through January 2023, utilizing 
22 
the Company’s updated Metrics Manual provided in 
23 
Confidential Attachment 6 to DPS-356. 
24 
4351


--- Page 128 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
34 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s recommendation to maintain the 
1 
current metric targets utilize the traditional 
2 
methodology? 
3 
A. 
No, our recommendation does not.  
4 
Q. 
Why does the Panel recommend deviating from the 
5 
traditional methodology? 
6 
A. 
According to our analysis, using our traditional 
7 
methodology to calculate updated targets using a 
8 
five-year average and two standard deviations 
9 
would yield lower Call Answer Rate and Customer 
10 
Satisfaction Survey targets and a higher PSC 
11 
Complaint Rate target.  As such, this would 
12 
allow the Company’s customer service to 
13 
significantly backslide without incurring a 
14 
negative revenue adjustment.  Therefore, we 
15 
recommend the Commission maintain the Company’s 
16 
current targets for the three existing metrics 
17 
in the Rate Year. 
18

19

A.

2 
PSC Complaint Rate  
3 
Q. 
For the purposes of the PSC Complaint Rate, what 
4 
types of complaints are included in the metric? 
5 
A. 
Per our explanation in the CIS Billing 
6 
Transition section, the Department takes two 
7 
types of complaints: initial complaints and 
8 
escalated complaints.  Initial complaints are 
9 
escalated if the customer informs the Department 
10 
that the utility failed to satisfy their 
11 
complaint.  Only escalated complaints are 
12 
included in the PSC Complaint Rate metric, not 
13 
initial complaints. 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposal regarding 
15 
its PSC Complaint Rate metric. 
16 
A. 
In the Customer Experience Panel’s initial 
17 
testimony, the Company proposes to maintain the 
18 
current minimum target of 1.0 and the subsequent 
19 
tier ranges.  The Company also proposes to 
20 
exclude complaints related to commodity prices 
21 
from the calculation of its PSC Complaint Rate 
22 
metric. 
23 
Q. 
What is the Company’s justification for 
24 
4353


--- Page 130 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
36 
 
excluding complaints related to commodity 
1 
prices? 
2 
A. 
The Company states that complaints regarding the 
3 
topic of commodity prices are outside the 
4 
Company’s control and do not present a just 
5 
cause for charging a complaint against the 
6 
Company.  Commodity prices are a pass-through 
7 
cost to the customer by the Company and 
8 
fluctuate based on market conditions.  
9 
Additionally, the Company states on page 7 that 
10 
“this exclusion would increase alignment across 
11 
the state, as this exclusion was included in Con 
12 
Edison’s Joint Proposal recently approved by the 
13 
Commission.” 
14 
Q. 
Over the last five years, has the Company met 
15 
its performance target for the PSC Complaint 
16 
Rate metric?  
17 
A. 
The Company met its PSC Complaint Rate target 
18 
from 2018 through 2021.  However, the Company 
19 
did not meet its target for this metric in 2022.  
20 
Q. 
What does the Panel recommend regarding the PSC 
21 
Complaint Rate metric? 
22 
A. 
We recommend the Commission deny the Company’s 
23 
proposal to exclude commodity price-related 
24 
4354


--- Page 131 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
37 
 
complaints from the PSC Complaint Rate metric.  
1 
Q. 
Why should these complaints continue to be 
2 
included in the calculation?  
3 
A. 
We recommend the inclusion of these complaints 
4 
for several reasons.  First, the Company’s root 
5 
cause analysis categories, provided in 
6 
Attachment 2 of its response to DPS-355, do not 
7 
include complaints specifically attributed to 
8 
commodity prices; therefore, we conclude that 
9 
the Company has not evaluated its complaints to 
10 
determine whether the root cause is commodity 
11 
prices.  Thus, the Company has not demonstrated 
12 
that it can accurately determine whether 
13 
commodity prices are the root cause of a 
14 
complaint.  Second, eliminating commodity price-
15 
related complaints from the metric calculation 
16 
would not encourage proactive communication by 
17 
the Company to its customers regarding commodity 
18 
prices. 
19 
Q.

20

21

22

Q.

Estimated Bills  
15 
Q. 
How is an “estimated bill” defined in the CSPI 
16 
Order? 
17 
A. 
The CSPI Order states an “estimated bill occurs 
18 
when the utility renders a bill based on an 
19 
estimated reading because it did not obtain an 
20 
actual reading at the time of a regularly 
21 
scheduled meter reading.” 
22 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any comments regarding the 
23 
Company’s estimated bills? 
24 
4366


--- Page 143 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
49 
 
A. 
The Company has been routinely estimating usage 
1 
every other month for customer bills since it 
2 
transitioned from bimonthly billing to monthly 
3 
billing.  For the rate of estimated bills 
4 
reported on the CSPI reports, only the estimates 
5 
that were scheduled to be actual readings are 
6 
counted.  
7 
Q. 
How is the estimated bill metric calculated?  
8 
A. 
The estimated bill metric is a percentage.  This 
9 
percentage is calculated by dividing the number 
10 
of estimated bills, as defined in the CPSI 
11 
order, by the number of scheduled actual 
12 
readings.  
13 
Q. 
What is the Company’s performance on estimated 
14 
bills? 
15 
A. 
In the CSPI reports filed by the Company in Case 
16 
15-M-0566, the Company’s annual average for the 
17 
Estimated Bills metric was 3.2 percent for 2018, 
18 
2.4 percent for 2019, 6.9 percent for 2020, 8.9 
19 
percent for 2021, 8.9 percent for 2022, and 4.3 
20 
percent for January through September of 2023.  
21 
We note that the Company’s performance on this 
22 
metric is not currently subject to an NRA. 
23

What does the Panel recommend regarding

10 percent or greater of bills are issued with

1 
potential NRAs associated with the Company’s 
2 
CSPI performance? 
3 
A. 
We recommend the Commission set the potential 
4 
maximum total NRA at 72 basis points beginning 
5 
with calendar year 2024 performance, as this is 
6 
when the Rate Year would begin. 
7 
Q. 
Why does the Panel recommend increasing the NRAs 
8 
prior to the start of the Rate Year? 
9 
A. 
The Rate Year is not aligned with the calendar 
10 
year; however, we recognize customers’ 
11 
frustrations with the Company’s customer 
12 
service.  Given that customers’ frustrations are 
13 
widely known, the Company already has ample 
14 
notice that it needs to improve its customer 
15 
service performance as soon as possible and 
16 
should be taking actions to do so, which will 
17 
help it meet the targets we recommend.  
18 
Therefore, it is our position that the increased 
19 
NRAs should take effect for the calendar year in 
20 
which the Rate Year begins, rather than in 
21 
calendar year 2025 in which the Rate Year ends. 
22 
Q. 
What does the Panel recommend for NRAs 
23 
associated with each metric?  
24 
4375


--- Page 152 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
58 
 
A. 
We recommend that the Commission establishes 
1 
NRAs set at a maximum of 18 basis points for 
2 
each metric: PSC Complaint Rate, Customer 
3 
Satisfaction Survey, Percentage of Calls 
4 
Answered within 30 Seconds, and the newly 
5 
proposed Estimated Bills. 
6 
Q. 
What is the dollar value of a pre-tax basis 
7 
point for the Company? 
8 
A. 
As a result of Staff’s recommended equity ratio 
9 
and level of rate base for the Rate Year, as 
10 
demonstrated in the Staff Cost of Capital 
11 
Panel’s direct testimony, the value of one 
12 
combined basis point equals approximately $0.161 
13 
million.   
14 
Q. 
What is the dollar value of the NRAs the Panel 
15 
is proposing? 
16 
A. 
Using a maximum of 18 basis points for each 
17 
metric, or 72 basis points total, the combined 
18 
total NRA values would be $11.6 million.  While 
19 
this represents a significant dollar value 
20 
increase, we reiterate that the Company’s 
21 
customer service levels have significantly 
22 
deteriorated since its billing transition, as 
23 
discussed earlier.  We are concerned that the 
24 
4376


--- Page 153 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
59 
 
NRA amounts established in the 2021 Rate Order 
1 
do not provide the Company with an adequate 
2 
incentive to maintain acceptable levels of 
3 
customer service. 
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel have a recommendation for when 
5 
the basis point value is determined for a metric 
6 
period? 
7 
A. 
We understand that basis points can vary over 
8 
any measurement period.  In order to avoid 
9 
confusion and to ensure all parties are aware of 
10 
the dollar amount exposure for the calendar 
11 
year, we recommend that the Commission require 
12 
the Company to include the dollar amount of a 
13 
combination basis point for the applicable year 
14 
in the Company’s annually filed CSPI report.  
15 
Q. 
Should any incurred NRA accrue interest? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  Consistent with current practice at other 
17 
utilities, in the event the Company incurs an 
18 
NRA, we recommend the balance accrue interest at 
19 
the pre-tax return rate from the time it is 
20 
recorded on the Company’s books at the close of 
21 
the year, until such time as the Commission 
22 
rules on the disposition of the balance, which 
23 
would likely be in the Company’s next rate case. 
24 
4377


--- Page 154 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
60 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any additional 
1 
recommendations regarding the Company’s NRAs? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  We recommend that the Commission reinstate 
3 
the tripling and quadrupling provisions for 
4 
customer service NRAs when the Company’s 
5 
performance results in it incurring an NRA in 
6 
multiple years.  The Commission adopted this 
7 
provision in its June 26, 2013 Order Authorizing 
8 
Acquisition Subject to Conditions as part of the 
9 
Company’s merger with Fortis, Inc. in Case 12-M-
10 
0192, Joint Petition of Fortis Inc. et al. and 
11 
CH Energy Group, Inc. et al. for Approval of the 
12 
Acquisition of CH Energy Group, Inc. by Fortis 
13 
Inc. and Related Transactions.  The Order 
14 
states, “During a period of dividend restriction 
15 
under the financial provisions of the Joint 
16 
Proposal, the maximum NRA would be increased 
17 
even further ... if performance targets were 
18 
missed three times in any five-year period.”  
19 
Given the Company’s customer service performance 
20 
on all three of its metrics after the billing 
21 
transition, we consider the re-establishment of 
22 
these tripling and quadrupling provisions 
23 
appropriate in this rate proceeding, as this 
24 
4378


--- Page 155 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
61 
 
would create an even stronger incentive for the 
1 
Company to improve its performance.   
2 
Q. 
Do you recommend any modifications to these 
3 
provisions? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  We also recommend the Commission modify 
5 
these provisions so that the NRAs would triple 
6 
if the targets for two metrics are missed in one 
7 
year, and then quadruple if one or more metric 
8 
targets are missed the following year.  For 
9 
example, if the Company misses its target for 
10 
the Call Answer Rate metric in calendar year 
11 
2024 but meets performance on its other metrics, 
12 
it would be subject to the standard NRA for the 
13 
Call Answer Rate metric with no tripling.  If 
14 
the Company misses two targets, e.g., for the 
15 
Call Answer Rate and PSC Complaint Rate metrics, 
16 
in 2024, the value of both missed NRAs would 
17 
triple for 2024.  If the Company then meets its 
18 
targets for those two metrics in calendar year 
19 
2025, but misses its Call Answer Rate 
20 
performance target in that same year, the NRA 
21 
for this missed metric would quadruple because 
22 
it would be the second year in a row in which 
23 
the Company missed a CSPI target.  
24

62 
 
Adjusted Bills  
1 
Q. 
Please define an “adjusted bill.” 
2 
A. 
According to the Commission’s definition set 
3 
forth in the CSPI Order, an adjusted bill is 
4 
“... a second or subsequent bill that is 
5 
rendered in order to correct a previously 
6 
rendered bill for service.” 
7 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any comments regarding the 
8 
Company’s Adjusted Bills? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  The Company’s percent of adjusted bills 
10 
rendered to customers to correct billing errors 
11 
significantly increased after September 2021 as 
12 
a result of the CIS billing transition.  
13 
Compared to pre-transition, the amount of 
14 
adjusted bills remained at higher levels 
15 
throughout 2022.  Although the number of 
16 
adjusted bills has been decreasing so far in 
17 
calendar year 2023, it has not yet dropped to 
18 
the levels seen before the billing system 
19 
transition. 
20 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any recommendations 
21 
regarding Adjusted Bills? 
22 
A. 
Yes.  We recommend the Commission require the 
23 
Company to implement a customer bill credit of 
24 
4380


--- Page 157 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
63 
 
$20, similar to the existing Missed Appointment 
1 
credit and our proposed Estimated Bill credit.  
2 
The Commission should require the Company to 
3 
apply this credit for each instance that it 
4 
renders a bill with an adjustment.  We also 
5 
recommend that the Company implement this bill 
6 
credit at the shareholders’ expense in the start 
7 
of the Rate Year. 
8 
Q. 
What is the rationale behind this 
9 
recommendation? 
10 
A. 
The Company has had an ongoing issue with its 
11 
CIS and has been attempting to reduce the number 
12 
of adjusted bills sent to customers.  The 
13 
implementation of a $20 bill credit for each 
14 
adjusted bill would provide an immediate and 
15 
consistent incentive for the Company to ensure 
16 
that its bills are rendered correctly the first 
17 
time.  Additionally, the $20 credit will be an 
18 
immediate form of relief that is visible to the 
19 
customer.  Furthermore, the credit from 
20 
shareholders will show goodwill to affected 
21 
customers on the part of the Company. 
22

Q.

24

Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
91 
 
associated with Residential Terminations and 
1

2 
A. 
The Residential Terminations and Uncollectibles 
3 
Incentive Mechanism offers the Company an 
4 
opportunity to earn a positive revenue 
5 
adjustment, or PRA, by reducing residential 
6 
customer service terminations and uncollectible 
7

8 
Niagara Mohawk, could also incur an NRA for poor 
9

10

6 
Community Distributed Generation Billing 
7 
Q. 
Please describe community distributed 
8 
generation. 
9 
A. 
Community distributed generation, or CDG, allows 
10 
for large-scale electric generation, such as 
11 
solar panels, and enables the owner of this 
12 
generation to share potential benefits, such as 
13 
bill credits, to the participants.  The 
14 
generation is owned and, potentially, operated 
15 
by a single entity, often called a sponsor or 
16 
developer.  Participation in these programs 
17 
allows for the benefit of clean energy 
18 
generation to be passed on to a variety of 
19 
prospective participants, consumers, or 
20 
subscribers.  These participants can be renters 
21 
or homeowners, low- and moderate-income 
22 
customers, or even businesses. 
23 
Q. 
Please describe what is required of utilities 
24 
4411


--- Page 188 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
SCSP 
 
 
94 
 
regarding community distributed generation 
1 
customer billing. 
2 
A.  In October 2017, the Commission approved the 
3 
Order Establishing Oversight Framework and 
4 
Uniform Business Practices for Distributed 
5 
Energy Resource Suppliers, which created Uniform 
6 
Business Practices for providers of CDG and on-
7 
site generation, in Case 15-M-0180.  In the 
8 
Order Regarding Consolidated Billing for 
9 
Community Distributed Generation, issued 
10 
December 12, 2019, in Case 19-M-0463, the 
11 
Commission directed the Joint Utilities, which 
12 
includes Central Hudson, to initiate 
13 
consolidated billing for CDG customers.  The 
14 
Order also directed the utilities to file 
15 
implementation plans by February 2020 that 
16 
detail the anticipated timelines for the 
17 
implementation of net crediting and cost 
18 
estimates.  The Commission granted an extension 
19 
of time for filing the Sponsor Net Crediting 
20 
Agreement and Net Crediting Manuals and 
21 
submission of tariff leaves to effectuate net 
22 
crediting until September 1, 2020.  Central 
23 
Hudson filed its Net Crediting Manual on August 
24 
4412

21

Q.

8 
A.

10

11

18

19 
within the Mark J. Holtermann (Information 
20 
Technology) direct testimony from the Company’s 
21
